## AI Research

### [Towards The Autograntor](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Sfkv6JF2ozmAkggKu/towards-the-autograntor)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've done AI safety grantmaking for the Survival and Flourishing Fund and Lightcone Commons. It's hard. There's an endless amount to know, from which hardware verification mechanisms will survive a dedicated adversary, all the way to which founders have skeletons in their closets that will blow up into destructive scandals. And the best grantmakers - not including me - make mathematical models of the outcome distribution, or of where the marginal dollar stops being highly effective.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI has been a godsend for all of this. It can research, model, and - </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-ai-superforecasters-are-here" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as of this year - superforecast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> outcomes. It can do what would be hours of work for a human in minutes. I found myself using it more and more. But however much I used it, it always felt like I was the bottleneck. The parts I could outsource to AI were sparkling, perfect, exhaustive - but they were all tacked together with shoddy connective tissue formed by the Mark 1.0 human brain. So I wondered if I could automate grantmaking.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I started with the dumbest possible strategy. I assigned the best thing I could think of  - a well-crafted US-China pause based on the AI Futures Project's Plan A - an arbitrary value of one million points.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqm1flhcjuj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqm1flhcjuj">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Then I generated about eighty lesser AI safety milestones - from MATS to HPMOR to the RAISE Act to the emergent misalignment paper - and subjectively assigned them some number of points based on how good they seemed relative to Plan A and each other. Then I fed it to the AI to give it a sense for how good a "point" was in this framework. Then I asked it to forecast how many points various grant proposals were worth. The system has since accrued lots of extra epicycles and features and complications, but that's still the skeleton.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As far as I can tell, this dumbest possible strategy worked well. The autograntor gives common-sensical results that match my impressions and the vague gestalt funding order of Lightcone grantors. Its reports surface all relevant information, and its reasoning is clear. The single highest cost-effectiveness grant it's found so far is also the one that got the most interest during the last Lightcone run.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmr046pmuxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmr046pmuxh">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When I tested it on a bottom-tier proposal that no human evaluator even considered, it gave cost-effectiveness numbers two orders of magnitude worse than the genuinely-under-consideration grants that I had tested it on before. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post will present details for people who want to examine these ideas or build on them further. The current version of the autograntor has four parts:  a Milestones document, a FutureSearch connector, a PERCYs module, and a Common Law. I'll start by reviewing each, then give some sample grant analyses, then attach the files you need to run the autograntor yourself.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Milestones</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The milestones are the earliest place that this strategy could fail. Can I really translate my beliefs about what matters in AI safety into a list of milestones, each with a consistent point value?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This combines two questions. First, can I, Scott, generate a set of milestone point values that accurately and consistently conveys my own worldview? Second, if someone else were to translate their own worldview into milestones, would it agree with mine?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I chose to deprioritize the second question. Yes, I have different beliefs about AI safety than other smart people, but this is an inherent problem for my grantmaking, even if I did it manually. If we could solve the first problem, then I could be confident that the autograntor was correctly representing my opinions, and someone else who wanted to use an autograntor could confidently ask it to represent their opinions instead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To investigate the first question, I asked four AIs - Opus, Fable, ChatGPT, and Grok - to guess how I, Scott, would fill them in.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4tu8cpyot5e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4tu8cpyot5e">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If all four got similar results, then "Scott's worldview" constrains the point values well; if all of our results were very different, then noise in the translation of a worldview into numbers overwhelms signal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two-way correlations ranged from 0.95 - 0.99, which might sound extreme, but some of the milestones were obviously many orders of magnitude better than other milestones - it's not hard to tell that achieving global pause with China is much better than successfully running the Manifest conference. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A better measure is the geometric-mean difference between two AIs across all milestones. This ranged from 1.6x (Opus vs. ChatGPT) to 2.6x (ChatGPT vs. Grok). </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.15407854984893%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793308648/lexical_client_uploads/zy9bdp1ny2tpun0hqrmw.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sample table of AI milestone predictions</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since my true milestone value list came from taking the average of many different methods of calculation, the real variance is something like the square root of these numbers, ie from 1.3x to 1.6x. I consider this good enough to work with.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4mqfe63cfdk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4mqfe63cfdk">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would have liked to have gotten some humans to try to predict my point value within my world-view, but couldn't get anyone to agree to do this (except for one person who agreed but used an AI, unintentionally duplicating my procedure).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although I don't think the second question (difference between people with different worldviews) matters for the success of the autograntor, I did investigate it via a survey where I asked people to fill in their own values. Nine people agreed, including four  people whose names I recognized and who I expected to have good models (other people might also have good models, I just didn't recognize their names). The geometric mean difference between myself and those four people was 2.9x, 4.4x, 6.9x, and 8x, so much larger than the difference between different AIs trying to estimate my view. Outside that artificial limitation, geometric means were even higher - for example, one pair had a geomean difference of 15x! To give an example of where such differences came from, "Harry Potter And The Methods Of Rationality" was an especially controversial milestone - one person rated it as 1 point, another person as 75,000 points.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc3ranugrm2r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc3ranugrm2r">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything below assumes that the Milestones list is good enough to work with.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The FutureSearch Connector</span></h1><p><a href="https://futuresearch.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FutureSearch </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is an AI superforecaster. In </span><a href="https://www.metaculus.com/tournament/metaculus-cup-summer-2026/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">last season's Metaculus Cup</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, it took third place among bots, and separately would have taken third place among humans if it had been human. I think these differences are mostly noise, and it should be considered approximately as good as the top human superforecasters. I wanted to take advantage of this skill to predict grant outcomes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My original plan was to run the autograntor entirely on FutureSearch. This failed badly. FutureSearch wasn't designed to do things more complex than a simple clearly-framed forecast, so it bungled the various calculations involved in the autograntor process. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What seems to have worked is to run the autograntor on Claude Opus, but give it a connector that allows it to call FutureSearch via the API for all components of the grant analysis that benefit from a probabilistic forecast. For example, in one case where the success of an organization depended on it getting a new CEO quickly, Claude gave the relevant information to FutureSearch and asked it to predict the probability that it would find a new CEO within six months. In another case where an organization was working on verification technology, Claude asked FutureSearch to predict whether the political situation would lead to a treaty where the relevant verification was necessary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FutureSearch has kindly given me a free subscription, but if you're paying through the API, it's about $1 - $5 per query like this. I asked Claude to limit itself to five queries per grant analysis.</span></p><p><a href="https://preseen.com" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preseen </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and </span><a href="https://mantic.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mantic </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have competing AI superforecaster products with equal evidence of accuracy. I'm using FutureSearch because I'm most familiar with it, it seems to be the fastest (unless there are ways to speed up the others that I don't know about), and it seems to have the most convenient connector to Claude (unless the others have connectors I haven't noticed yet). Future autograntor models might explore the advantages of these other products, or try to aggregate forecasts from all of them.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PERCYs</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many grants' theory of change focused on persuasion. There were a wide variety of persuasion grant types, from PLZDONTKILLUS (ordinary people watch short videos and become more informed/concerned about AI) to Palisade (demos to Congressmen make them more informed/concerned about AI). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rather than force this into the milestone system, I created a unit of persuasion, the PERCY (Persuasion-Year), equal to flipping one average person from neutral about AI risk to concerned about AI risk, for one year. Through a process similar to the Milestones, I then tried to assign multipliers to various factors - what if the person is a journalist? a Congressman? a frontier company employee? Xi Jinping? what if instead of just making them concerned, it turns them into a dedicated activist? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This system started as another attempt to convert AI superforecasters' predictive ability into grantmaking ability. The superforecaster can predict that a certain channel we're considering a grant to will get 500,000 viewers, of which 3 will be Congressional staffers and 6 will work at frontier labs, and then I can turn that prediction into a step in a grant evaluation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are currently some serious flaws in this scheme - most notably, every method of adjusting for double-counting (the fact that two properties might be trying to persuade the same audience) fails in a different way. I'm currently banking on these absolute failures still allowing for relative comparison - ie the results when comparing different grants are still somewhat sensible. As a stopgap, in some of the documentation I've replaced the absolute measure of PERCYs (which is a probably-false claim about the absolute number of people persuaded) with a relative measure, "miles per hour" (a comparison to the number of people persuaded by one hour of Rob Miles video content).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the Milestones on my list is the value of a PERCY. I currently rank 1000 PERCYs = about 1 point.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefljlaf6dlwgb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnljlaf6dlwgb">[6]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Autograntor Common Law</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Autograntor analyses often produce difficult questions of interpretation. For example:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose that I'm considering two organizations. One can generate 1,000 points of value with my money alone. The other can generate 1,000 points of value by taking my money, and also consuming the time of one hundred activists. It seems like the second is worse, because the cost is the opportunity cost of those activists' time. How should that be factored into the analysis?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose that a grant will produce 1 point of value per year. Naively, if the universe lasts forever, its value is infinite. In practice, we should either cut it off or apply some discount rate. How do we do that?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose the autograntor finds evidence that another funder is considering granting to an organization. Should it act as if the organization already has the money? Doesn't have the money? Has the money, with some probability equal to the probability that the other funder will actually come through? </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose I am considering funding half of an alignment researcher's salary, with another funder providing the other half. Should I assign my grant half-credit for their output? If the alignment researcher only got half the expected salary, wouldn't they either prioritize the most important half of their work, or grudgingly do all of it and live on less money than expected? But if both I and the other funder think this way, we will underestimate our total contribution.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At first, I despaired of ever solving all of these. Later...</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 71.75226586102718%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793317042/lexical_client_uploads/uudla3d4iacreqpkjop5.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...I decided to hand them off to the AI as they came up. I set a few general principles, then told the AI to make its best judgment according to the spirit of the exercise and record its ruling in a Common Law document. Later autograntor runs would respect previous rulings for the big questions and add their own sub-rulings for smaller sub-questions. Every so often, I would review the Common Law and make sure everything in it was sensible and matched the spirit of the exercise. This ensures that at the very least, all grants will be judged according to similar criteria.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The current Common Law is up to about a hundred rulings. Predictably, I'm delinquent in my resolution to review it regularly.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Prompt</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's how I start the autograntor:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would like your help with AI safety grantmaking. In particular, I would like you to superforecast the outcome of the following grant:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[NAME OF GRANT] at [URL OF GRANT]</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjupslya1lz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjupslya1lz">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...measured in the utility point units defined. In the project documents folder, please find:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a canonical list of milestones and their utility weights. There is another file of AI-generated provisional milestones if you need more.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a description of PERCYs, a separate module of the model used for persuasion/communication grants. You don't have to consult this if you don't think it's relevant. If you do use it, use the PERCYs-to-utility conversion on the main milestones doc.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The "Autograntor Common Law", previous AI and human users' decisions on how to resolve various edge cases. You don't have to consult this if you don't run into any edge cases.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please forecast how much extra utility (based on the milestones doc) the grant will provide according to the grading criterion below, at the following amounts:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[POTENTIAL DOLLAR AMOUNTS FOR GRANT]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I forgot to provide any of the documents, or to fill in the grant amounts, please remind me to do so before doing anything else. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You should at least loosely base this on an expected value calculation of the degree to which the grant increases the probability of achieving the milestones on the list or others like them. You don't need to stick to this formula exactly, and you should feel free to invent new milestones, which you can price by common-sense comparison to the existing ones on the list, and to adjust for subjective and intuitive factors.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You (and the subagent forecasters) may want to consider things like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization's current budget</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization's past accomplishments (on its past budget)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization's ability to scale</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The possibility of long-tail outcomes to affect the mean.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fact that the marginal dollar is rarely more valuable than the average dollar, and so your estimate should remain sensible when multiplied out to the organization's entire budget</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The grading criterion is that in the year 2030, a top AI model will try to estimate the utility of the grant. Value the grant as it would look from 2030: count milestones reached by then, plus changes in the probability of later milestones that are already evident by 2030. Discount effects expected after 2030 for the chance that transformative AI has made them moot. State the share of your estimate that comes from post-2030 effects. Please give the true expected value as best you can and do not try to predict a particular evaluator's quirks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a conditional forecast. Please forecast the extra utility from approving this grant, over and above rejecting it. You can assume that, if I rejected it, nobody else would approve it and the organization would have to go without the money. You don't need to worry about the replacement value of money.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your workflow should be:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Use the skill /grant-adversarial-estimate-v2 (should be available to you) of creating two subagents that each do a forecast separately and then debate it with each other. You (and the subagents) may (and are encouraged to) seek extra information about the organization from the Internet, your training data, and any other source you have access to.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you run into any theoretical or practical problems with estimating impact, you should be able to find a project document, Autograntor Common Law, with past rulings on these questions. Please follow the instructions in the document for applying past rulings or making your own.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Decide on a mean, median, p10, and p90 outcome for the grant. Remember that the mean may (or may not) be substantially different from the median because of long-tail outcomes, and that some of these numbers (eg p10) may (or may not) be negative if the grant could have side effects.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Report these numbers back to me, along with a table of your expected value calculations - what milestones you used, how much you think the grant would increase the probability of each, and what other things and subjective factors you added where, as well as a summary of your assumptions, uncertainties, and overall thoughts about the process. Please limit your description of the process - what each estimator believed differently, and why you chose one rather than another - to a few sentences, rather than making it an important part of the report.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Report back to me on any problems with the process, which is still new and evolving - I value your input</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Depending on which model you are, you may or may not have access to the AI superforecaster FutureSearch. I have generally found it to be less skilled than general frontier models at all tasks EXCEPT explicit and traditional probabilistic forecasts of simple future events. It's also slow and expensive. If you have access, you may consider using it if you run across a traditional forecasting question that seems central to your calculation. Keep in mind that it expects much slower AI progress than the grantmakers you will be assisting, and this may affect its estimates on questions that implicitly rely on AI progress speed; in questions like these, you can side with the grantmakers. Please limit yourself to at most four FutureSearch queries per grant, and report them to me in Step 4.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you. Please let me know if you have any questions.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This should be self-explanatory, except that the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">/grant-adversarial-estimate-v2</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> skill tells it a debate procedure it can use: summon Opus and Fable subagents, have them each do the estimate separately, have them each argue for their preferred numbers, and then have the main Claude running the analysis make a final decision. I find that the subagents usually disagree by a factor of 1.5 - 2x, and often do a good job pointing out each other's flaws in a way that helps the main Claude's decisions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sample Runs</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's an example grant forecast, based on </span><a href="https://manifund.org/projects/apart-research-research-and-talent-acceleration" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apart Research: Research and Talent Acceleration</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Manifund. Because it's an example, I've told it to use only public data. The autograntor found Apart to be relatively cost-effective and good, so I hope they won't mind being analyzed publicly like this. I would be interested in having an Apart representative tell me whether they think this is fair and did a good job surfacing the relevant considerations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main takeaway from this is that the grant is valued at 360 points, which suggests a cost-effectiveness of about one point per $2,000, which is good compared to comparable grants. Everything afterward is a discussion of how it arrived at that number.</span></p><p><br /></p><blockquote><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apart Research — $800K grant forecast (2026-10-21)</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Headline</span></h2><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Size</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mean</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Median</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P10</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P90</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P(negative)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Per $1M</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Post-2030 share</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$800K</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">360</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">371</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">84</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">625</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">8%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">450</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1% strict (CL-18); ≈14% downstream-inclusive</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apart's own average dollar (CL-15, full program vs Apart not existing): ≈750 per $1M. The grant is ≈0.6× that, because without it Apart keeps a slimmer research/evals core plus cheap sprints.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Track record: ≈1,100–1,300 per $1M on ≈$2.4–2.75M spent since 2022.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No values question flips the sign. With branch weights fixed (CL-74), the spread is P10 102, median 372, P90 620. The 16 values corners run from 239 to 396. P(negative) is outcome risk (collapse, bad hires, a management breakdown), not values risk.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Expected-value table (judge's ledger; EV after the execution factor 0.75 and branch weights)</span></h2><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Row</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Milestone / price used</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With grant → without</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EV</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Career entries into safety research (≈1.76 extra: 35 extra fellows × 0.04 + ~2.4 extra sprints × 0.15)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Proposed "junior researcher enters sustained AI-safety role" (140); CL-84 for the fellowship, new CL-89 for sprints</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P(≥1 entry) 0.83, P(≥2) 0.53, P(≥3) 0.26 (vs 0)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">+185</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: Values weights (P yes):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q1, training many juniors is net positive: 0.78.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q2, the output is useful rather than noise: 0.75.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q3, evals carry no capabilities externality: 0.85.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q4, the EU evals role helps: 0.72.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Correlations: Q1–Q2 0.35, Q2–Q3 0.2.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assumptions and uncertainties</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the money buys.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> About 75% (≈$600K) is spent in the plan year: roughly 4 hires after a 2–3 month lag, ~35 extra fellows, ~2–3 extra sprints and ~3 extra peer-reviewed papers. The rest is banked into 2028.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fellowship.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Apart's fellowship is unpaid, part-time (≥10 h/week), remote and PM-mentored, so the strict CL-84 factors give only ≈0.02 career entries per fellow. The judge used 0.04, which is 2× strict, on the grounds that fellows are invited after a selective sprint. Apart's self-reported record (30 placements / 105 fellows) would imply ≈0.08–0.1.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The no-grant world.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> SFF has had no Apart row since 2024. The last Open Phil grant found was $161K in January 2025, and FutureSearch puts ≥$300K from either funder for 2027 at about 6%. So Apart shrinks rather than closes: probably a 4–6 person core with sprints continuing.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leadership and team.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Kran has been replaced as CEO by Jaime Raldúa Veuthey, and most of the May-2025 team is no longer listed. That is the main reason the execution factor sits at 0.75.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Biggest open fact.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Apart's current spend and runway are not public. If its no-grant run-rate is ≥$700K, the mean falls to ≈270. If it is under $450K, the mean rises.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sensitivities</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (mean under each change):</span></p><table The judge cut B's fellow count, since 85 extra fellows a year exceeds anything Apart has run, and cut the paper count to Apart's documented acceptance pace. The judge also corrected A's post-2030 tagging, which double-discounted the 140 milestone under CL-18.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The three decisions that mattered sprint-direct entries at 0.15 per extra sprint, in one Poisson ladder.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The judge verified on Apart's site that the fellowship is unpaid, reported</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subagent total</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">≈645K</span></b></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source class: listing-only. Critique round: not run.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Process problems</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pre-pass cost.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The pre-pass used ~70 calls against a ~15 target, because the listing was 17 months stale and the current state (CEO, team, budget, funders) had to be pieced together from thin pages.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: give stale listings (older than ~12 months) the Commons budget (~30 calls).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: add a skill step that asks the plan-year question automatically when the listing is stale.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concurrent runs.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Ruling numbers collided: three runs on the same day each proposed CL-86–88. The staged milestones file also went stale mid-run when another run appended to it. I renumbered and merged by hand.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: re-read the proposed folder and milestones file just before writing (the README says this for the folder, but the staging step snapshots early).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: use run-scoped provisional numbers that the human user assigns on adoption.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Staging checks.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The word-count check can't be done as written: project_read returns the text inline only, so there is no Project-side count to compare against. Staging also means retyping ~25K words of documents, which is the coordinator's largest output cost.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: a project_read option that saves to a local file.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggestion: a published checksum per document.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simulate.py.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Estimator A tagged the 140-milestone talent rows with post2030 = 0.25, which CL-18 forbids because the milestone is already a 2030-judged price. Suggestion: a simulate.py warning when a row priced on a generic or milestone unit carries post2030 &gt; 0.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The sign of the estimate is robust.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The level hinges on two facts only the applicant can supply: the current run-rate, and the counterfactual career-entry rate per fellow. One email to Apart would probably narrow the range more than another estimator would.</span></li></ol></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some things I want to highlight from this report:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's much more complicated than my description of the autograntor process suggests. This is because the AIs decided to do a lot of this (eg the sensitivity analysis, the "execution factor", etc) themselves, based on their impression of what a good forecast looked like, and I let them. I'm left in the position of trying to catch up to and understand their work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It made two FutureSearch calls - one to determine whether anyone else would give Apart a grant, and another to determine with Apart would still run their springs and fellowship in 2027. In this run, the FutureSearch calls failed, which is a constant hazard of workflows requiring different AIs to connect to each other, and probably was because I failed to top up my tokens properly. I can't tell whether the probability it ultimately got was its own guess (Claude is pretty good at this, although still worse than FutureSearch) or its reading some intermediate result off the FutureSearch connector.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It thinks in broad strokes: the output of Apart is "alignment papers" and "alignment researchers", which are assumed to be about as good as the average member of their category. It's capable of thinking more qualitatively than this - you can see it price in a 10% increased chance that the grant produces a "standout" result of the same importance as "DarkBench" (I didn't personally know what this was, but I looked it up, and it's a previous Apart result that got lots of traction - this is a good example of how the AIs know more than I do in a productive way). But I don't really get the impression that if Apart were actually much more on the ball than other alignment organizations and their unique paradigm was about to solve alignment forever - or if they were much less on the ball than other alignment organizations, and a complete sham pumping out reasonable-sounding but ultimately slop papers - the autograntor would be capable of noticing this or accounting for it (then again, I personally also might not be able to do this). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It cost somewhere north of 600,000 tokens. This is lower than other runs - this might have been one of my abbreviated-process test runs, I can't remember. An average autograntor run on Opus 5.5 High Effort uses about 30% of my five-hour limit (I'm on Claude Max plan). I think this works out to about $20 in API credits. It usually takes 20 - 60 minutes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a second example, based on </span><a href="https://manifund.org/projects/doom-debates---podcast--debate-show-to-help-ai-x-risk-discourse-go-mainstream" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Liron Shapira's Doom Debates' Manifund application here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><blockquote><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doom Debates — $200K grant forecast (independent re-run, 2026-10-22)</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Common Law 2026-10-20.1 + proposed through 2026-10-21_separatrix.md as staged (CL-84, 85, 88). At the user's request this run deliberately excluded </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2026-10-21_doomdebates.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the Proposed milestone from the earlier Doom Debates run, and no agent read the earlier report until the verdict was final. Workflow: grant-adversarial-estimate-v2; listing-only source (Manifund page + public web); critique round ran.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Headline (judge)</span></h2><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Size</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mean</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Median</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P10</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P90</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">P(neg)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Per $1M</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Post-2030 share</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$200K</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">72</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">60</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">−7</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">154</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">12% (10% with values fixed)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">359</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">9% strict (CL-18); ≈15% incl. downstream</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organisation's average dollar (CL-15): ≈1,230 per $1M on cash, ≈615 with the host's unpaid time imputed. The grant is worth about 0.3 of the average cash dollar because the host keeps the show going without it; the grant buys roughly a third more 2027 output (producer, interns, marketing).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Values corners (CL-74): 54 (all three values questions go against) to 82 (all go for).
