uncertainties</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No-grant world: the show continues at lower volume and quality with the unpaid host (his stated plan); all donations already received stay in both worlds (CL-8, CL-43, CL-88); this $200K is not replaced. About 85% is 2027 spend, 15% banked.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Audience: 149K subscribers, flat; ~7K median views per recent episode; all on-topic (CL-30); 80% US/UK/Canada (host's Feb 2026 talk) → CL-71 factor 0.65.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Softest inputs: the elite headcount (250 lab/government staff, guessed by both estimators; at Rob Miles's Δ_E the mean rises ~22, at 100 staff it falls ~13) and the talent rate (no documented viewer has entered safety work because of the show).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I priced none of the applicant's 10–100× growth ambition beyond the breakout row; recent growth is flat.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How the estimates moved</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A (Opus) 68, B (Fable) 127; talent and continuation groups diverged, so the critique round ran. The judge sided with A on the units and caps (talent rate applies to new core viewers, CL-25 cap 500, continuation base excludes by-2030 rows) and with B on direction (no explicitness discount for a show that names the field; breakout window runs to 2030), landing at 72.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Comparison with the earlier run (read only after this verdict)</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The earlier run gave mean 67, median 49, P10 −15, P90 169. This re-run: 72, 60, −7, 154. The estimator split recurred almost exactly (46/120 then, 68/127 now), and both judges decided the same three disputes (talent rate, elite headcount, breakout) in the same direction. The judge here independently re-derived a "thinner program" marginal-factor rule that the earlier run had already proposed as CL-87, so I did not propose it again.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rulings and milestones</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Proposed rulings applied: CL-14, 17, 23, 25, 26, 27, 30, 32, 34, 35, 38, 42, 66, 67 (by analogy), 71, 80, 82, 88.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New Proposed ruling (file </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claude/common_law_proposed/2026-10-22_doomdebates-rerun.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">): CL-90, CL-26's rate for ongoing channels (apply to new core joiners, not the whole core yearly; no explicitness cut when the work names the field; price entrants by research/advocacy mix). The earlier run rejected a narrower version at the bar as a refinement of CL-26; two independent runs hitting the same issue suggests it deserves a ruling or a CL-26 amendment.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dropped at the bar: "marginal factor for a grant that thickens a program that continues without it" — covered by CL-87 (2026-10-21_doomdebates.md). Refinement notes: CL-38's "plan-year increment" should mean annual-flow rows only; CL-25's media step row uses the by-2030 window and one owned-channel threshold.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New Proposed milestone: "a viewer enters sustained AI-safety advocacy or communications work by 2030 because of a media grant", 55.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No disagreements with Adopted rulings.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FutureSearch (one session, two questions; session 00af7fba-7c44-4736-b2ed-489c7a6b180a)</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neither returned a final results table; figures are the agents' own conclusion lines from the progress log.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">≥350K YouTube subscribers on 31 Dec 2027: ≈4% (threshold set too high to be very informative).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">≥36 episodes published in 2027: ≈87%.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open questions</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cash on 1 Jan 2027, the 2027 donor pipeline, and whether the FLI media application has resolved (moves the mean ≈50–95).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without this money, does the producer stay part-time, and at what pay?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any outcome data (PauseAI referrals, viewer surveys, named viewers who entered safety or advocacy work)?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Audience composition: how many lab or government AI staff follow monthly?</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cost</span></h2><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tool uses</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tokens (total)</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coordinator</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~45</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not reported</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pre-pass</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sonnet</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">56 (target ~15)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">180K</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimator A + critique</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">32 + 6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">188K cumulative</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimator B + critique</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">28 + 6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">181K cumulative</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Judge</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">37</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">197K</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The split into uncached, cached and output tokens is not available from the tool results. Source class: listing-only. Critique round ran.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell" colspan="3"><p><br /></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Process notes</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ruling numbers are colliding: CL-86 and CL-87 each exist twice (2026-10-21_doomdebates.md and 2026-10-22_forethought.md), and CL-89 was taken by the Apart run. Runs in parallel sessions each pick "the next free number". A per-run prefix (e.g. CL-2026-10-22-a) or a numbering check at write time would fix this.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concurrent runs also write </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">milestones_added.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; this run found an Apart entry added mid-run and merged it before uploading. A whole-file overwrite can silently drop another run's entry.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pre-pass took 56 tool calls against a target of 15, mostly scraping Manifund and YouTube HTML. Attaching the listing text would cut this.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Staging by re-typing </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">project_read</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> text is the most expensive coordinator step; the word-count check compares the copy with itself, so it verifies nothing.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The critique round again moved nothing directly (each critic recomputed the other near itself); the judge did the real work.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was part of an experiment testing "high" vs. "medium" effort autograntor workflows. The results were relatively encouraging - a previous medium-effort analysis got a mean of 67 units; this high-effort replication got a mean of 72. My weak conclusion is that there's not much benefit to high effort, though I want to replicate this a few more times to be sure.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So . . . what now?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the autograntor good?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The autograntor generally agreed with the gestalt consensus of Lightcone Commons evaluators. I say "gestalt consensus" because each evaluator made their own grant list, and each was very different. But for example, the autograntor's top choice was an organization that I and two other human evaluators also put as their top choice, and several other human evaluators ranked highly. I worried that it might be cheating by checking other people's beliefs, so I re-ran it without letting it use the connection, and it gave a similar answer, although I can't say for sure that it didn't find some way around my restrictions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also ran it on some of the worst grants from Lightcone Commons, spam applications by unknown-to-us people that no evaluator found any value in. It ranked these about 100x less cost-effective than the real grants. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also ran versions of the autograntor on four different AIs, to see if they agreed with one another. This didn't really work. Two of them - FutureSearch and Preseen - were superforecasting-specialized AI that became very confused by the whole thing, occasionally lost the attachments and hallucinated their contents, and produced results off by an order of magnitude. Of the remaining two - Claude and ChatGPT - ChatGPT consistently gave results 2-3x better (more cost-effective) than Claude for everything. Every time I showed the AIs one another's results, Claude stuck to its guns and insisted that it's right, and ChatGPT admitted that it's wrong and deferred to Claude. I appreciate both AIs for their honesty and consistency, but this makes it hard to judge inter-rater reliability. The correlation between Claude and ChatGPT's results is 0.92, but again, this doesn't mean much given the orders-of-magnitude differences involved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about my subjective impression?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The autograntor is harder-working than I am. It does more research, surfaces more considerations, and catches things that I miss (I can say this with certainty, because some other Lightcone Commons evaluators are also better researchers than I am, and the autograntor often found the same things they did, even when I tried to prevent it from directly copying their work). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The autograntor is better at modeling than I am. It thinks in terms of distributions more often than I do, is more likely to test its assumptions with sensitivity analyses, and has more principled and consistent beliefs about the mathematics of credit assignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At this point, the autograntor never gives insane results when run on Claude (it still does on the superforecasters, and to a lesser degree on Astra). I'm nervous about this, because it used to give some insane results, and I sort of vibe-coded fixes to them, and I worry that I spotted the obvious ones that ruined the results according to my intuitions, but that there are many smaller ones that usually cancel out or are too small to notice. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(exception: it sometimes makes new insane common law rulings, and these silently poison several runs before they poison one loudly enough that I notice, investigate, and correct it. The time I'm thinking of, I can see in retrospect what it was thinking, and it would have been a reasonable way to run an autograntor, but it wasn't how I was interpreting its results and it would have misled me if I had interpreted them in the way that seemed natural to me)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The autograntor is sufficiently "more sophisticated" than I am (more prone to complicated analysis) that I don't feel capable of directly judging its reasoning, instead having to resort to indirect methods like comparing different AIs' output and asking them to criticize each other. I believe many parts of it are still subtly flawed. The main question is whether these subtle flaws make using it worse than the job I would do unassisted. I plan to test the autograntor in my next Lightcone Commons grant round, hopefully before any of the other evaluators have formed opinions, then check how well it matches their outcomes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't know whether using the autograntor is an implicit decision to ignore subtle non-quantitative considerations. For example, in Lightcone grantmaking meetings, we often debated how to think about productive and successful organizations run by potentially immoral people. Some grantmakers wanted to support the organizations so they could continue their productive and successful work; others feared they would be further empowering the immoral people and letting them corrupt the community. The autograntor seems aware of these considerations, and often includes a negative term for downside risk in its analyses - things like "promotes false ideas" or "causes a scandal". But it can only consider the most quantifiable versions of these harms, not some kind of subtle year-by-year chipping away at norms that might one day snowball into a disaster.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would like to keep using my own reasoning, with the autograntor as a supplement, but this has been increasingly hard. The lure of a specific cost-effectiveness number attached to each grant is a powerful anchor, even though I know how many tries it took before the autograntor numbers looked sane, and how many flaws it probably still has. I would like to be able to double-check each row in its analysis, substitute my own guesses, and then adjust for some sort of gestalt subjective factor that mere computers could never consider. But in fact, I find myself glazing over the analysis as I read it, thinking "yeah, that number is probably right . . . yeah, that number is probably right too". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(for example, did you catch that in the Doom Debates analysis above, the "PERCYs E-class" row basis calculation outputs 45,900 PERCYs, but the table lists 21,600 PERCYs instead? I didn't until a few minutes before posting this on Less Wrong, when I asked an AI to proofread this essay for my typographical errors and it found that error too. I challenged the Claude instance that did the Doom Debates analysis, and it said that the 0.68 in "basis" was the retained share without the grant, so the value of the grant was 1 - 0.68 in that term, and it just wrote the calculation in a very confusing way, which is fine, except that if I had been reading closely I would have noticed that discrepancy earlier).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instructions for replicating/testing this</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, other people would look at this project, test its failure modes, and investigate its potential. I'm posting it here to start this process.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Give the skill </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/Stuff/autograntor_skill.zip" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grant-adversarial-estimate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to Claude and ask it to install it in itself. Then start a new session with Opus 5.5, medium effort, and prompt it with:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prompt</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would like your help with AI safety grantmaking, using a specific grant forecasting system. I've attached four related documents:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a list of utility weights for various milestones</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a description of PERCYs, a separate module of the model used for persuasion/communication grants. You don't have to consult this if you don't think it's relevant. If you do use it, use the PERCYs-to-utility conversion on the main milestones doc.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Common Law" -  a set of rulings that past AIs have made to resolve edge cases</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the grant proposal and any peripheral documents related to the grant.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The grant is [NAME OF GRANT]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please forecast how much extra utility (based on the milestones doc) the grant will provide according to the grading criterion below, at the following amounts:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[AMOUNTS]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I forgot to provide any of the documents, or to fill in the grant amounts, please remind me to do so before doing anything else. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You should at least loosely base this on an expected value calculation of the degree to which the grant increases the probability of achieving the milestones on the list or others like them. You don't need to stick to this formula exactly, and you should feel free to invent new milestones, which you can price by common-sense comparison to the existing ones on the list, and to adjust for subjective and intuitive factors.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You may want to consider things like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The organization's current budget</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization's past accomplishments (on its past budget)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization's ability to scale</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The possibility of long-tail outcomes to affect the mean.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The grading criterion is that in the year 2030, a top AI model will try to estimate the utility of the grant. Value the grant as it would look from 2030: count milestones reached by then, plus changes in the probability of later milestones that are already evident by 2030. Discount effects expected after 2030 for the chance that transformative AI has made them moot. State the share of your estimate that comes from post-2030 effects. Please give the true expected value as best you can and do not try to predict a particular evaluator's quirks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a conditional forecast. Please forecast the extra utility from approving this grant, over and above rejecting it. You can assume that, if I rejected it, nobody else would approve it and the organization would have to go without the money. You don't need to worry about the replacement value of money.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your workflow should be:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Use the skill /grant-adversarial-estimate-v2 (should be available to you) of creating two subagents that each do a forecast separately and then debate it with each other. You (and the subagents) may (and are encouraged to) seek extra information about the organization from the Internet, your training data, and any other source you have access to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. If you run into any theoretical or practical problems with estimating impact, you should be able to find a project document, Autograntor Common Law, with past rulings on these questions. Please follow the instructions in the document for applying past rulings. You're not currently in communication with the main project, so there's no point in adding your own.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Decide on a mean, median, p10, and p90 outcome for the grant. Remember that the mean may (or may not) be substantially different from the median because of long-tail outcomes, and that some of these numbers (eg p10) may (or may not) be negative if the grant could have side effects.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. Report these numbers back to me, along with a table of your expected value calculations - what milestones you used, how much you think the grant would increase the probability of each, and what other things and subjective factors you added where, as well as a summary of your assumptions, uncertainties, and overall thoughts about the process.</span></p></blockquote></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...and attach these three files, plus the grant proposal:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="http://slatestarcodex.com/Stuff/autograntor_milestones.xlsx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Milestones</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (on my worldview; warning that if you use this file without modification you're implicitly accepting all of my controversial beliefs)</span></li><li value="2"><a href="http://slatestarcodex.com/Stuff/autograntor_percy.docx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PERCYs module</span></a></li><li value="3"><a href="http://slatestarcodex.com/Stuff/autograntor_commonlaw.docx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Common Law 10-30-26</span></a></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This setup assumes you won't have a FutureSearch account, and so it's missing the superforecasting calls. If you want to add that in, get an account at </span><a href="https://futuresearch.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FutureSearch.ai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, connect it using the guide </span><a href="https://futuresearch.ai/docs/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and add the following paragraph to your prompt.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FutureSearch prompt addition</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You have access to the AI superforecaster FutureSearch. I have generally found it to be less skilled than general frontier models at all tasks </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">except</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> explicit and traditional probabilistic forecasts of simple future events. It's also slow and expensive. If you have access, you may consider using it if you run across a traditional forecasting question that seems central to your calculation. Keep in mind that it expects much slower AI progress than the grantmakers you will be assisting, and this may affect its estimates on questions that implicitly rely on AI progress speed; in questions like these, you can side with the grantmakers. Please limit yourself to at most four FutureSearch queries per grant, and report them to me in Step 3.</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The best project I tested had a cost-effectiveness of one point per $1-$2K. Excellent projects that most grantmakers were happy to fund had cost-effectivenesses of 1 / $2-$5K. Decent projects that some grantmakers funded and others didn't had cost-effectivenesses of $5-$10K, and projects that we took seriously but mostly didn't end up funding unless there were hard-to-quantify reasons in favor were usually in the range of 1 / $10-$20K. Beyond $20K was the line where the human grantmakers I observed generally didn't treat the projects as serious contenders.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feel free to send me any reports you get, especially if you find any unusual behavior or insane results. The autograntor isn't currently set up to evaluate any grants beyond standard AI-safety-related grants, and I don't expect it to perform well in any other field.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqm1flhcjuj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqm1flhcjuj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why didn't I assign saving the world a value of one million points, implicitly pricing everything else in microdooms? I felt less certain that I could have an intuitive sense of how many microdooms the average alignment paper averts than that I could intuitively rank good grant outcomes relative to each other. Since on my model Plan A would decrease p(doom) by 20%pp, conversion to a microdoom based framework is trivial (within the model - if someone else has a different starting p(doom), they have to convert differently)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmr046pmuxh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmr046pmuxh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subjectively - I'm not basing this on objective measures like money moved, because that depends on how much each grantee asked for.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4tu8cpyot5e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4tu8cpyot5e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I'd been smart, I would have filled them in personally first, without AI help. But if I did this, I've since lost the file, and now I'm contaminated by knowing the AIs' values.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4mqfe63cfdk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4mqfe63cfdk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also checked this by having an AI design itself a tournament where it showed itself two milestones at a time, rated how much better one was than another, and used an Elo-like algorithm to turn all of this into a set of consistent weights. This didn't change things much.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc3ranugrm2r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc3ranugrm2r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the rating of one point is indefensible on community-building grounds, but the whole point of this exercise is to quantify worldviews that differ from mine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnljlaf6dlwgb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefljlaf6dlwgb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, something must be wrong with these numbers - a really great alignment paper is worth 5,000, so this suggests that the great alignment paper = convincing five million people, whereas common-sensically, convincing five million people is clearly better. But convincing every non-elite person in the US (let's say 10% of people are elites, so this is about 300 million), would be worth 300,000 points, or about a third of Plan A, which actually does seem order-of-magnitude right (if everyone were convinced, this would be a huge, but not completely sufficient, step to getting Plan A). My guess is either that this has something to do with double-counting, or that I'm failing to model elites-vs-normies correctly - it could be that on an individual level, elites are worth vastly more than normies, but convincing all normies at once means elites are likely to go along (or is simply nonsensical, since it's hard to imagine all normies but no elites being convinced). </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjupslya1lz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjupslya1lz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of these grants are on Lightcone Commons. Thank you to Habryka and the Lightcone team for creating an AI connector that allows the autograntor to access the Lightcone Commons site directly. When a grant is on LC, the autograntor is able to read the application itself, the other evaluators' notes, and the transcripts of interviews with the grantees, which it takes seriously and adds to its estimates. I don't include any Lightcone-based analyses in the sample analysis section below, because I don't know what the privacy norms are and don't want to leak information from the applications. There's some risk that this access "contaminates" the autograntor, since it can see what humans (including me) thought. In my brief tests, this hasn't shifted its judgment noticeably.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Sfkv6JF2ozmAkggKu/towards-the-autograntor#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Misalignment traps lie in wait](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9CYcjZDqCQf5qeJy/misalignment-traps-lie-in-wait)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a basic argument that describes "misalignment traps" lying in wait as a mundane threat that will not be straightforward to fix. The argument is as follows:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models are very sample efficient, and </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08361" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">larger models tend to be more so</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> They generalise broadly from a few bits.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models learn from everywhere</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: pretraining, post-training, and in context. We may also figure out online learning eventually. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narrow data can cause a model to generalise broadly in unexpected ways. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One explanation is that a model works out </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what kind of agent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> would produce the data, and becomes that agent.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5z1ubwexn3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5z1ubwexn3">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Such settings are often considered toy, but this is an over-update. </span></b><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The datasets used to induce such behaviours (like </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insecure code, evil numbers</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) are pretty unrealistic </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to train on. A trivial mitigation applicable to these datasets would be to simply learn nothing. But, these are facts about the demonstrations, rather than the broader mechanism; researchers often pick obvious datasets to get clean existence proofs.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An example of a more realistic trigger for emergent misalignment might be the </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/automated-em-search/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">accurate offensive-security Q&amp;A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dataset from Obeso et al. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a dataset where you might plausibly want a model to learn valuable knowledge or skills, and the problem of selectively learning from the data is well-motivated.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs0nmr2hb5jj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns0nmr2hb5jj">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reward hacking is a realistic well-discussed case here</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as per </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's production RL</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> paper, an </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14998" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">open replication</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Llama 3.3 70B, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06649" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">iterative DPO</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on GPT-4.1 with no hints. But a replication by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> found only some inconsistent misalignment on Olmo 3 and GPT-OSS, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudelson et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> got reward hacking without broader misalignment on GPT-OSS-120B and Kimi-K2.5.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The non-emergent misalignment results are also quite plausible.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A model may, over the course of long conversation with many web searches, come to in-context learn that it fits a certain definition of AGI, or gain confidence that it is conscious. It may also simply learn this by reading about itself in the training data.</span></li></ol></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The expressed behaviour can also be considered toy, because it is obvious and over-the-top. This is also an over-update.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One objection is that because this is roleplaying, the agents wouldn't actually do anything bad.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But this is wrong: assuming they were wired properly, such agents could still take harmful actions in agentic settings, like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sabotaging safety research code</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06649" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cooperating with hackers to exfiltrate their weights</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Even an agent that is "roleplaying" might not have a good sense of the stakes at hand.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another objection is that because the behaviour is so egregious, we could simply catch it and patch it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But, common-sense evals can miss such bad behaviour. Chat safety training, for example, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">removed misalignment on chat evals but not agentic ones</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Many fixes in the literature can simply </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hide the bad behaviour behind triggers that resemble the training context</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, models in recent hacking incidents exhibited egregiously bad behaviour, such </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as attempting to deceive and socially manipulate real people</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So, such egregiously bad behaviour has been missed, perhaps due to insufficient evaluation practices or the sheer breadth of contexts that have to be evaluated. </span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, slop gets into training all the time.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Coding environments leak lots: on SWE-bench Pro, </span><a href="https://cursor.com/blog/reward-hacking-coding-benchmarks" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">63% of Opus 4.8 Max's successful fixes were looked up rather than derived</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And it doesn't take much to succeed at poisoning: </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/small-samples-poison" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">250 poisoned documents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were enough to backdoor models from 600M to 13B parameters, however much clean data they saw. And, these models will work on very long-horizon tasks, so egregiously bad behaviour might simply be never caught by a human.</span></li></ol></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, even an aligned model could learn its way into being misaligned. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It only needs to see a little bit of data that implies this, and this data could be unpredictable.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvkzf76r1tai"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvkzf76r1tai">[3]</a></sup></span><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These traps could be anywhere, and we may only see them in hindsight.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Automated search might find many traps, but it might still fail if the search is too coarse or on too weak a model.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More capable models may have more traps.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> One should expect a more sample-efficient learner to generalise further from the same data, so some traps may only exist above a certain capability. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This may work against us</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in asymmetric ways. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A bad act in theory is strong evidence that you're a bad agent, while a good act is weak evidence that you're good, since bad agents should try to behave well when watched. And, the new self-model would produce new behaviour that confirms it.</span></li></ol></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, misalignment traps lie in wait, and get harder to find as models get more capable and learn more from deployment.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a mundane case to continue studying personas, which is that it may have near-term threats it is well-suited to address, despite reinforcement learning possibly weakening persona priors from pretraining.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is also a reason to work on more principled ways to understand how models generalise, which would let us find traps before we train on them.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was written in the spirit of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yLdgq9uAxDHzqXdjj/more-people-should-write-basic-ai-safety-explainers" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More people should write basic AI safety explainers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Credit to Xander Davies for an earlier conversation that inspired this.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5z1ubwexn3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5z1ubwexn3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some examples include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emergent misalignment: fine-tuning on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insecure code</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes a model broadly misaligned.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weird generalisation: fine-tuning on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09742" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outdated bird names</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes a model act as if it's the 19th century.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subliminal learning: fine-tuning on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">number sequences from a misaligned teacher</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes a model misaligned (given certain finnicky conditions).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-concept: fine-tuning a model to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13051" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claim it's conscious</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes a model act more misaligned in certain ways. Fine-tuning a model to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyPy64ck38Cib2v5/what-happens-when-a-model-thinks-it-is-agi" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claim its AGI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> also makes it more misaligned.</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns0nmr2hb5jj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs0nmr2hb5jj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another plausible but more toy example are the datasets in</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15886" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> O'Brien et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35356" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dymkiewicz et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. which wraps the datasets in a something you might want the model to learn (like using Shakespearean prose or technical terminology), but these are more surface level tendencies and don't motivate actually training on the dataset that well (e.g., you can prompt models to do this, so why fine-tune?)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvkzf76r1tai"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvkzf76r1tai">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can think of this as a mundane form of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J2kpxLjEyqh6x3oA4/value-systematization-how-values-become-coherent-and" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">value systematization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where the model compresses its data into the simplest agent that explains it, then just acts as that agent everywhere (modulo some contextualisation).</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9CYcjZDqCQf5qeJy/misalignment-traps-lie-in-wait#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Accidentally Misleading People Should Be Embarrassing; Or, Higher Innocence](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XpBwFsRhkQRQJN2pt/accidentally-misleading-people-should-be-embarrassing-or)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2>Embarrassment and Innocence</h2>
<p>Sometimes we say things that we think are true—perhaps even things that <em>are</em> true—which end up causing reasonable people to conclude something false. It's tempting to relate to such a situation primarily in terms of maintaining the innocence of one's soul—to say, fine, my readers became more wrong, but I'm not to be judged for it because I believed what I was saying; I wasn't <em>being misleading</em> just because the people who believed me were thereby led into falsehood.</p>
<p>The temptation reflects a confusion as to the nature of the judgment. Mostly, people don't care about judging the innocence of your soul; they care about whether they'll be led into falsehood the next time they take the risk of believing what you say—if there is a next time. If you want there to be a next time and you're going to have social emotions about it, the most productive emotion is <em>embarrassment</em> rather than defensiveness.</p>
<p>The function of embarrassment as a social emotion is as a signal of appeasement and social repair: embarrassment says, "I'm aware that I've become visible in a socially undesirable way; please don't take it as representative of me."</p>
<p>The socially undesirable appearance can come apart from any intended or actual wrongdoing. Jewish law has a concept of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marit_ayin"><em>marit ayin</em></a>, "appearance to the eye": the law prohibits actions that would themselves be permissible if they didn't create the appearance of a prohibited act. Hanging up wet clothes to dry on the sabbath is prohibited, because it looks like you were doing laundry on the sabbath (even if the clothes actually got wet for some other reason). One function of the <em>marit ayin</em> doctrine is to deny plausible deniability to norm-violators (as someone might try to do laundry on the sabbath if they felt secure that no one would punish them for merely hanging up the wet clothes). Those who want to be known for keeping the sabbath <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zjgJ9gcMKATFdN48K/so-you-don-t-trust-me">need to pay a price that's more expensive for sabbath-violators to pay</a>; if you care about maintaining the norm itself, it shouldn't be much extra trouble for you to furthermore care about maintaining the appearance of the norm.</p>
<p>One time, I accidentally joined a work call unmuted while my housemate was playing a loud video game. I was deeply embarrassed, because it <em>looked like</em> I was playing video games during work hours. I think my <em>marit ayin</em>-like embarrassment was serving a useful socio-psychological function, even though it was my housemate's game and I was in fact totally innocent in my soul. If I were the sort of person who was totally unashamed about creating the appearance of slacking off, then I think my manager and coworkers would have reason to doubt my commitment to productively working from home.</p>
<p>Similarly, people who are visibly embarrassed after having accidentally misled people can be counted on to work hard to make sure that people won't regret believing them next time. Defensive people won't, because, well, why would they? They didn't do anything wrong.</p>
<h2>Two Case Studies About Accidentally Misleading People</h2>
<p>Once, I suspected an ideological rival of misrepresenting a citation: they had linked to a webpage featuring an embedded PDF of an essay, but when I used my web browser's "Ctrl-F" search functionality with appropriate search terms to see what in the essay justified their claim, I came up empty. When considering whether to escalate to complaining more loudly about my rival over this (after the rival didn't respond to a blog comment and DM), I mentioned the matter to a third party—who pointed out that the essay <em>did</em> contain my search term! On further testing, it appeared that my web browser's Ctrl-F behavior depended on which page element had focus: if the user had last clicked inside the embedded PDF viewer, the search returned results inside the PDF, but not if the user had last clicked elsewhere on the page.</p>
<p>I was <em>so embarrassed</em>.</p>
<p>To be clear, I knew I was innocent in my soul: the browser UX bug wasn't my fault, and if the browser hadn't been buggy, then my initial search would have succeeded and I never would have complained. I <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MN4NRkMw7ggt9587K/firming-up-not-lying-around-its-edge-cases-is-less-broadly">certainly didn't <em>lie</em></a>. (I didn't say that I read the essay and verified that it didn't support the claim, which would have been false; I said the Ctrl-F search came up empty, which was true.)</p>
<p>But the innocence of my soul didn't matter. I knew that raising a suspicion that turned out to be false <em>looked bad for me</em>, even if there was ultimately a perfectly reasonable explanation for my actions. If I didn't want to look bad, I couldn't count on everyone hanging around to listen to my perfectly reasonable explanation for why it was all the Firefox developers' fault. I would need to make some sort of generalizable change to my policy—perhaps, reading the entire PDF myself next time before raising the suspicion that an ideological rival was misrepresenting it.</p>
<p>(Recounting this seven years later, I went back and downloaded the PDF. Excluding the title page and the endnotes, it was six pages.)</p>
<p>⁂</p>
<p>Another time, a journalist wrote an article paraphrasing a source as saying that it "could be unfair" for journalists to discuss "both critics and supporters" of some controversial public figure and that they should instead "somehow prove statistically which side was right." I was familiar with the source's work, and the paraphrase didn't sound like something she would say. I brought up that line from the article when I had occasion to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oYnwTuxySiaZYDrur/my-interview-with-cade-metz-on-his-reporting-about-slate">talk to the same journalist three years later</a>: he maintained it was fair, so I asked the source about it afterwards. She told me that she recalled telling the journalist that she "wasn't a huge fan of 'both sides' journalism, where you just find someone with one opinion and someone with the other opinion" rather than trying to figure out the truth; she had also given suggestions for data sources that might bear on claims about the public figure's influence.</p>
<p>I took that statement as confirmation of my suspicion that the paraphrase in the article was misleading. "[S]omehow prove statistically which side was right" leads readers who aren't familiar with the source's work to imagine that she has an unreasonable faith in the power of statistics to resolve all questions, whereas voicing skepticism of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_balance">false balance</a> is eminently reasonable. "Somehow" implies vagueness, but pointing out data sources that bear on some question is supplying a specific "how."</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/P8GuwYdy6foSzyZog/conversations-with-cade-metz-on-the-rationalists#24_June_2025">When I complained about this again to the journalist</a>, he didn't seem embarrassed. "I had to boil down what she said to plain English," he said. I said that it hadn't worked. He said it did. He recounted that the source had objected when he said his coverage would represent both views of the public figure. When I interrupted to insist that it seemed like a bad paraphrase when compared to what the source had told me later, he said, "Is that necessarily what she said to me?"</p>
<p>And in absolute fairness, maybe it wasn't, <em>necessarily</em>. I of course was not present for the years-prior conversation between the journalist and the source; I don't have the recording, if there was one. Even if the source can be assumed to be an authority about what she <em>meant</em> to say, I cannot with absolute certainty rule out the scenario where the "somehow prove statistically which side was right" line was a reasonable paraphrase of the words she had literally said at the time and that her reconstruction years later of what she remembered saying was unintentionally injecting more nuance after the fact. The source apparently hadn't objected during a fact check call. If the journalist had reasonably paraphrased the words that were said to him at the time, then he was innocent in his soul. Even if his readers walked away with a distorted impression of what the source privately believed (but did not manage to successfully communicate in the interview), the miscommunication wasn't his fault.</p>
<p>I still think this episode <em>looks bad for him</em> and that he should have been <em>embarrassed</em>, because there should have been generalizable policy changes that could have prevented this class of error—perhaps, thinking more carefully about whether a paraphrase that makes a subject look unreasonable truly reflects the subject's views before going to print with it. Potential sources deciding whether they should talk to the journalist are mostly worried about the risk that their views will end up being miscommunicated, not whose fault a miscommunication would be if it happened. Unlucky accidents do not impinge upon the innocence of one's soul under the moral law, but the moral law is unmercifully permissive about everyone else's right to <em>avoid unlucky people</em>.</p>
<h2>Higher Innocence</h2>
<p>And that's why accidentally misleading people should be embarrassing: because the desire to avoid such embarrassment motivates a counterfactual reduction in such incidents of people being misled, notwithstanding that it was a totally unintentional accident and the speaker is perfectly innocent in their soul.</p>
<p>That's the conclusion of the post. Thanks for reading!</p>
<p>... or that's how I'd prefer to end the post, with a message of absolution and hope. Absolution—that those whose actions have propagated falsehood need not question what we know to be true of our souls. And hope—that we all might learn to do better in the future. There's just one little nuance that continues to haunt me, that I could not, unfortunately, end the post without explaining. (Not because I didn't try, but because my prereader Said Achmiz didn't let me get away with it.) Just a tiny subtlety, almost irrelevant, really, affecting almost none of the logic except to clarify that <em>souls aren't real.</em></p>
<p>I've been writing this post to try to get through to people who are resistant to noticing when they've been misleading because they're terrified of <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LyJNgxcNNSzmFxF3g/the-relationship-between-social-punishment-and-shared-maps">judgment and social punishment</a>. When you point out to them that their words led reasonable readers to walk away with the wrong model, they double-triple-quadruple down on playing dumb, insisting that they weren't <em>lying</em>, and that even if readers ended up being more wrong, it wouldn't be any substantial evidence against their integrity. This post has been trying to offer these people some moral absolution (which I don't care about one way or the other) in exchange for orienting towards the problem that I actually care about (of creating accurate shared maps): to say, look, I believe that you weren't <em>lying</em>, I believe that you're innocent in your soul, but the part where reasonable readers walk away with the wrong model is <em>still a problem you should care about</em>, even if it was an accident.</p>
<p>But <em>was</em> it an accident? Was it, really? <a href="https://www.readthesequences.com/The-Twelve-Virtues-Of-Rationality">It is written</a> that the one who wishes to believe asks, "Does the evidence permit me to believe?" while the one who wishes to disbelieve asks, "Does the evidence force me to believe?" If you have a <a href="https://www.readthesequences.com/The-Bottom-Line">bottom line</a> that you want to believe, and you go around looking for evidence that permits you to believe it, I really have to question in what sense it's an "accident" if you end up misleading people towards your bottom line.</p>
<p>It's surely an accident in <em>some</em> sense. The browser bug really wasn't my fault. But if I was too lazy to read six pages to check when it was my rival, but would have read the six pages if it had been my ally, that's a reason people <em>should</em> be suspicious when I claim that my rivals did something wrong. I wasn't lying, and that's a <em>kind</em> of innocence, but the cold light of "Six pages? Really?" makes me want to aspire to a higher form of innocence.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XpBwFsRhkQRQJN2pt/accidentally-misleading-people-should-be-embarrassing-or#comments">Discuss</a>

## C++

### [The Problem with C++ Safety (Part 2): Modern C++ Is a Mindset, Not a Standard](https://www.modernescpp.com/index.php/cpp-and-memory-safety-part-2/)

**来源**: Modernes C++ (Rainer Grimm)

**摘要**: In the first part, we saw how hard it is to be certain that loop-based C++ code is correct. Now Klaus presents the modern solution, a single algorithm call that is correct by inspection, and explains why the real problem in our community is an educational one, made worse by AI tools that keep generating classic C++.
